- **evidence:** hyperparameters.json model_size=small_deep; model/config.py:143 small_deep feature_extractor_type='spectral_temporal'; model/preprocessing.py:113-115 linear interpolate with no anti-alias; model/config.py:180-185 fb config comment 'the training-script key reader + DataLoader zero-pad are wired at the retrain cutover (M4)'; model/feature_extractor.py:441 PhysicalFilterbankTokenizer implemented but unblessed.
- **fix:** Complete the M4 retrain of small_deep_fb and bless it as the headline release, or at minimum publish a rate-robustness curve for the current weights and document the rate assumption. Add a rate sweep (x0.5/x2/37 Hz) parity test so the released weights' invariance is measured, not assumed.
- **files:** model/config.py, model/preprocessing.py, model/feature_extractor.py
- **verify:** CONFIRMED(repro): All three primary evidence points independently reproduced. (1) hyperparameters.json at training_output/semantic_alignment/small_deep_v2_4b3fdd6/ explicitly reads feature_extractor_type: "spectral_temporal" — the blessed checkpoint uses the rate-dependent tokenizer. (2) model/preprocessing.py:114-115 confirms F.interpolate(mode='linear', align_corners=False) with no antialias kwarg; the finding's description of the call is exact. (3) The "bins map to different physical Hz" framing is imprecise — after interpolation to 64 samples over 2 s, bin k always maps to k*0.5 Hz, not rate-dependent. The true mechanism is aliasing: linear interpolation without an anti-alias filter folds frequencies above the interpolated Nyquist (16 Hz) into lower bins with different magnitudes depending on native rate. An empirical broadband test (2 Hz + 18 Hz signal across 25/50/100/200 Hz native rates) showed alias-bin energy at 14 Hz ranging from 3.0 to 24.9 — an 8x swing for identical physical content, confirming rate-dependent spectral representations. (4) grep across all training_output/**/hyperparameters.json returns zero matches for physical_filterbank; no .pt file matches fb/filterbank; the PhysicalFilterbankTokenizer is implemented in model/feature_extractor.py:441 but has never been trained or blessed. (5) The config comment at model/config.py:183 claiming M4 deferral of DataLoader wiring is stale — the wiring already exists in training_scripts and multi_dataset_loader.py — but this only means the fix is closer than implied, not that the finding is wrong. Core claim is intact: released weights use the aliasing tokenizer and no rate-invariant checkpoint exists. || REFUTED: The finding's core mechanism is empirically false. It claims the spectral_temporal tokenizer indexes rfft bins by sample count, so a fixed bin maps to different physical Hz at 50 vs 100 Hz, breaking variable-rate deployment. But patches are fixed-DURATION, not fixed-sample-count: preprocessing.py:46 sets patch_timesteps=int(sampling_rate_hz*patch_size_sec) (50 samples/1.0s@50Hz vs 100 samples/1.0s@100Hz), and BOTH are interpolated to target_patch_size=64 before the rfft (feature_extractor.py:331, n=fft_size=64). After interpolation a fixed-duration window maps bin k to k/patch_size_sec physical Hz, independent of native rate. Direct empirical test: a 2 Hz sinusoid windowed at 1.0s and interpolated to 64 samples peaks at bin 2 = 2.00 physical Hz for BOTH fs=50 and fs=100. The loader comment (multi_dataset_loader.py ~592) documents this intentional design: 'Legacy CNN/spectral path interpolates rate away'; the channel-text suffix '(sampled at Xhz, Y.Xs window)' additionally injects rate as explicit conditioning. Thus the released small_deep weights DO provide approximate physical-Hz rate invariance (spectral branch via time-window+interpolation; temporal branch via same interpolated patches + AdaptiveAvgPool1d), supporting the variable-rate story through a different mechanism than the unblessed PHz filterbank. It is true the PHz filterbank is implemented-but-untrained (config.py:180-185) and that linear interpolation applies no anti-alias filter on downsampling, but (a) that residual aliasing concern is not the bin-vs-Hz defect the finding asserts, and (b) HAR motion energy (<~15 Hz) sits far below any effective Nyquist here, making it a minor LOW-severity documentation/robustness note rather than the claimed MEDIUM design break.

## LIKELY

### [LOW] [design:tokenizer] No per-recording gain normalization: absolute-scale features become a unit/device fingerprint that will not transfer zero-shot
- **area:** tokenizer/normalization
- **issue:** The tokenizer preserves absolute amplitude (good for within-dataset intensity) but normalizes only with a single global frozen mu_k/sd_k per band plus one unstandardized amplitude scalar. A single global per-band constant cannot remove a per-dataset/per-device gain or unit offset, so the same motion recorded in g vs m/s^2 (or on a differently-gained device) sits a near-constant ~3-4.5 nats above in every band and in the amplitude scalar. That absolute-scale offset is effectively a dataset/unit fingerprint and, at zero-shot, a new device with unknown gain shifts all tokens systematically. The single mu_k/sd_k is also shared across acc/gyro/mag, so it is dominated by whichever channel type is most common and mis-centers the others.
- **evidence:** model/feature_extractor.py:516-517 single per-band mu/sd buffers; :722 e_hat=(e-mu)/sd; :725 amp=log1p(sum E) unstandardized. Measured same-motion window g vs m/s^2: mean per-band log-energy offset ~2.96 nats (std 1.72 across bands), amplitude-scalar offset 4.56 nats; log(9.81^2)=4.567.
- **fix:** Add per-recording/per-channel gain normalization the design already lists as an ablation but does not default to: PCEN or a robust per-window/per-channel scale (e.g. divide band energies by a running per-channel energy estimate) so intensity is expressed relative to that channel's own recent level; and/or standardize the amplitude scalar with its own frozen stats and fit norm stats per channel-type. Expected benefit: keeps relative-intensity cues while making tokens invariant to device gain and unit convention, which is central to the cross-device deployability thesis.
- **files:** model/feature_extractor.py
- **verify:** CONFIRMED(repro): All three sub-claims independently verified on real pamap2 data using the actual PhysicalFilterbankTokenizer class from the repo. (1) Code: norm_mu/norm_sd are (n_bands,)-shaped buffers at lines 516-517, shared across all channel types (acc/gyro/mag) by broadcast in the line-722 standardization. (2) The amplitude scalar at line 725 is log1p(E.sum) with no standardization applied. (3) Numerical measurement on real pamap2 accel windows: amplitude scalar offset g vs m/s² = 4.560 ± 0.011 nats, matching the theoretical log(9.81²)=4.566 almost exactly; per-band log1p(E) offsets averaged 4.12 nats (finding claims 2.96—directionally confirmed; the higher value on real pamap2 reflects vigorous-activity data with E >> 1 approaching the theoretical ceiling). (4) Fitting the frozen mu/sd on g-unit data leaves a residual of 4.06 sigma on m/s² inputs, confirming the offset is NOT removed by the current design. The finding's core claim—that absolute scale is preserved as a device/unit fingerprint in both the per-band features and the amplitude scalar, and that the frozen global constant cannot neutralize a per-device gain shift—is reproduced quantitatively. Minor discrepancy: per-band mean is 4.12 (not 2.96) on pamap2; this is because vigorous-activity band energies are large and the log1p offset approaches log(α²); lower-amplitude data would give smaller means. This does not contradict the finding—it shows the offset can be as large as the theoretical max. Scope note: the deployed headline checkpoint uses FixedPatchCNN/spectral_temporal with per-patch z-score, which removes the scale offset; the vulnerability is in PhysicalFilterbankTokenizer, the planned filterbank redesign for the next submission. || UNCERTAIN(repro): Code claims verified: feature_extractor.py:516-517 has a single (n_bands,) norm_mu/norm_sd; accumulate_norm_stats (:678-679) reshape(-1, n_bands) pools stats across acc/gyro/mag channel types; :722 standardizes per-band only; :725 amp is unstandardized. The 4.5-nat g-vs-m/s^2 offset math (log(9.81^2)=4.567) is correct, and the core mathematical claim (a single global per-band constant cannot remove per-recording/per-device gain) is true and not compensated downstream (tokenizer output goes straight to a Linear proj). I cannot refute that fact. HOWEVER the finding's flaw-framing and value are substantially undermined: (1) It is a documented, intentional design choice — design_tokenizer.md:48 explicitly offers PCEN as the recommended robustness ablation and :114 lists norm frozen/PCEN/off as a planned ablation; the finding itself concedes the fix is already listed as an ablation, so this is a design-priority disagreement, not a defect. (2) The proposed fix is partly self-defeating: 'standardize the amplitude scalar with frozen stats' subtracts another global constant and does NOT remove per-recording gain — the same critique it makes of the per-band mu; only the PCEN/per-window-scale half addresses gain, and that is exactly the existing ablation. (3) The dominant 4.5-nat term is the g-vs-m/s^2 unit convention, which is a KNOWN quantity already injected into the model via ChannelTextFusion per-channel unit/gravity text (DATA-17 manifests carry '(m/s^2, includes gravity)' / '(g, ...)'), so it is a text-signalled feature the model can learn to condition on, not an unknown fingerprint; the genuinely-unknown per-device calibration residual is typically <10% gain (<~0.1 nat), far below the headline number. (4) At 400h/26M, PCEN adds under-determined per-channel state/hyperparameters and its aggressive energy normalization fights the amplitude scalar whose explicit design purpose (design_tokenizer.md:49, Bottleneck 3) is preserving absolute intensity — defaulting to it could regress intensity discrimination. Also note the physical_filterbank tokenizer is only in SMALL_DEEP_FB_CONFIG, not the headline small_deep_v2 (spectral_temporal) checkpoint, so no reported result is currently affected. Net: technically-true observation, honestly documented as intentional, fix already an ablation and partly self-contradictory — a LOW-severity design suggestion rather than a MEDIUM flaw.

### [LOW] [design:objective] soft_target_weight=1.0 (pure soft targets) is the shipped value but is the worst of the three settings in the repo's ablation — the positive pair gets no dedicated hard-target mass, weakening alignment and confusable-class discrimination
- **area:** infonce-targets
- **issue:** SOFT_TARGET_WEIGHT=1.0 (semantic_alignment_train.py:300; hyperparameters.json soft_target_weight=1.0) means targets = 1.0*soft + 0.0*hard (semantic_loss.py:186), so the one-hot positive diagonal contributes zero. The target row is the frozen-SBERT similarity softmax, which spreads mass across all semantically-near columns; with per-patch flattening many rows are near-identical, so the true positive receives only a small fraction of the mass and the loss barely pulls the exact IMU/text pair together and barely separates confusable neighbors (e.g., walk-upstairs vs walk-downstairs). A small non-zero hard weight restores a guaranteed positive-pull + hard-negative gradient while soft targets still prevent synonym false-negatives.
- **evidence:** WAVE2_RESULTS_SUMMARY.csv (all hard_neg queue, 80ep, val/unseen): weight=1.0 (P6_headline) 0.7093/0.5442 and (P1_hard_neg) 0.7164/0.5710; weight=0.5 (P7_soft_weight_0p5) 0.7427/0.5876; weight=0.0/pure-hard (P7_hard_targets) 0.7411/0.5726. Pure-soft (the headline) is worst on both metrics; weight=0.5 is best (+2.6-3.3pp val, +1.7-4.3pp unseen over 1.0).
- **fix:** Set soft_target_weight to ~0.3-0.5 (blend), keeping frozen-SBERT soft targets for synonym handling but restoring a dedicated positive/hard-negative signal. Cheap retrain flag; empirically +~3pp. Optionally anneal weight upward over training.
- **files:** training_scripts/human_activity_recognition/semantic_alignment_train.py, training_scripts/human_activity_recognition/semantic_loss.py, training_output/runpod_ablations/WAVE2_RESULTS_SUMMARY.csv
- **verify:** CONFIRMED(repro): Independently reproduced from primary sources. semantic_alignment_train.py:300 sets SOFT_TARGET_WEIGHT=1.0 and the headline checkpoint hyperparameters.json confirms soft_target_weight=1.0, use_soft_targets=true (shipped value). semantic_loss.py:186-187 implements targets=(1-w)*hard+w*soft, so at w=1.0 the one-hot positive diagonal contributes exactly zero and targets are the pure frozen-SBERT similarity softmax — mechanism confirmed. In WAVE2_RESULTS_SUMMARY.csv, restricting to matched runs (all queue_mode=hard_neg, memory_bank_size=512, temp=0.07, 80ep, batch 32, verified per-run): weight=1.0 = P1_hard_neg 0.7164/0.5710, P6_headline s43 0.7093/0.5442, s44 0.7068/0.5334; weight=0.5 = 0.7427/0.5876; pure-hard (use_soft_targets=false) = 0.7411/0.5726. Pure-soft (shipped headline) is worst on BOTH val and unseen across all three of its seeds; weight=0.5 is best on both. Deltas of 0.5 over 1.0 are +2.6-3.3pp val and +1.7-4.3pp unseen, matching the finding. Caveats not affecting verdict: the pure-hard cell uses use_soft_targets=false (cross-entropy else-branch), not w=0.0 via the blend, though functionally equivalent; weight=0.5 and 0.0 are single-seed while weight=1.0 has three seeds, but all three weight=1.0 seeds fall below both alternatives so the gap is seed-robust and direction-consistent. Severity MEDIUM is accurate: a hyperparameter choice with a ~3pp effect fixable via a cheap retrain flag, not a correctness defect. || UNCERTAIN: Code facts are correct: SOFT_TARGET_WEIGHT=1.0 (semantic_alignment_train.py:300) and semantic_loss.py:186 targets=(1-w)*hard+w*soft, so at w=1.0 the one-hot diagonal weight is literally 0. But the finding's central empirical claim (1.0 is 'worst of the three settings' and blending buys +2.6-3.3pp) rests on a confounded, non-controlled comparison. The three cited settings are NOT one ablation: w=1.0 exists ONLY as P6_headline (seeds 43/44); w=0.5 (P7_soft_weight_0p5) and w=0.0 (P7_hard_targets) are P7-phase runs on seed 42. There is no P7 run at w=1.0 anywhere in WAVE2_RESULTS_SUMMARY.csv or the runpod_ablations directory. So the ~+3pp is a cross-phase P6->P7 delta (different phase base AND different seed), not a soft_target_weight delta. The ONLY controlled within-phase soft-target comparison is P7_hard_targets (w=0.0, s42) vs P7_soft_weight_0p5 (w=0.5, s42): 0.7411 vs 0.7427 val (+0.16pp) and 0.5726 vs 0.5876 unseen (+1.5pp) -- marginal, and the entire P7 family (tau_0p3 0.7441, channel_indep 0.7317, cnn_multi 0.7254, spectral_half 0.7144) sits ~3pp above P6 regardless of target config, indicating the phase change, not the weight, drives the gap. The mechanistic claim is also overstated: with frozen-SBERT soft targets, text_sim(i,i)=1.0 is the row maximum (lines 158-166), so after standardization+softmax the diagonal still receives peak target mass -- the positive IS pulled at w=1.0, softly not 'barely'; same-label off-diagonal mass is a genuine correct match (intended synonym handling), and for distinct confusable labels the diagonal still dominates. Additionally the finding ignores P1_none_s42 (0.7938/0.6578), the best model in the table by ~5-7pp, implying the queue/negative-mining strategy is a much larger lever than soft_target_weight. Not fully REFUTED because no P7 w=1.0 run exists to disprove a possible large penalty at 1.0 and the within-phase direction is weakly positive; but the finding's evidence does not establish its magnitude or its 'retrain-worthy MEDIUM lever' framing. Not addressed in the docs/v2 resolution log (that log covers DATA/MODELS, not infonce-targets). Corrected severity LOW: controlled evidence shows +0.16pp val / +1.5pp unseen, not +3pp.

### [LOW] [design:data-strategy] 20x rare-group oversampling collides with tiny subject pools, turning the classes balancing should help into subject-memorization traps
- **area:** sampling / subject diversity
- **issue:** compute_group_weights oversamples rare groups up to 20x, but the rare groups are exactly the ones sourced from datasets with very few subjects. Oversampling 480 'elevator' windows from 8 subjects (or 169-183 mhealth arm/leg/waist-exercise windows from 10 subjects) up to 20x means the model repeatedly sees these classes from a handful of people, so it can fit subject-specific idiosyncrasies rather than the activity. These are precisely the low-resource classes that generalization is supposed to help, so the imbalance fix undermines cross-subject and zero-shot performance for them. Oversampling is capped by frequency but never by subject count.
- **evidence:** semantic_alignment_train.py:310 max_oversample_ratio=20.0; multi_dataset_loader.py:775-847 compute_group_weights caps by group COUNT only, never subject count. Subject counts (capped corpus): mhealth 10, dsads 8, recgym 10, hhar 9, pamap2 9 vs capture24 151/kuhar 89/wisdm 51; 367 unique subjects total, with capture24+kuhar+wisdm = 291 (79%). 100%-single-dataset rare groups from tiny pools: arm/leg/waist_exercise (mhealth, 10 subj), elevator (dsads, 8 subj), push_up/sit_up/talking (kuhar).
- **fix:** Cap oversampling by the number of distinct subjects backing a group (e.g. weight ~ min(freq^-1, k * n_subjects)) or subject-stratify within each group so an upweighted class draws from as many subjects as it has, not the same few windows repeatedly. Report per-group subject counts alongside window counts. Expected benefit: rare-class exposure without subject overfitting, improving the cross-subject generalization the subject-disjoint eval measures.
- **files:** /home/alex/code/HALO/code/datasets/imu_pretraining_dataset/multi_dataset_loader.py, /home/alex/code/HALO/code/training_scripts/human_activity_recognition/semantic_alignment_train.py
- **verify:** CONFIRMED(repro): Independently reproduced. CODE: multi_dataset_loader.py:775-847 compute_group_weights sets w=count^(temp-1) then torch.clamp(max=min_weight*20) — caps purely by group WINDOW count, with zero reference to subject count; semantic_alignment_train.py:310-311,2253 wires MAX_OVERSAMPLE_RATIO=20.0, SAMPLING_TEMPERATURE=0.0 into it. DATA (recomputed from labels.json + _subject_of): per-dataset subject counts match exactly (dsads 8, mhealth 10, recgym 10, hhar 9, pamap2 9, capture24 151, kuhar 89, wisdm 51). Strongest examples reproduced exactly: elevator=480 win/8 subj (dsads 100%, raw 78x->capped 20x); arm/leg/waist_exercise=169/183/181 win/10 subj (mhealth 100%, raw 205-221x->capped 20x); ironing 193/8subj, vacuum_cleaning 142/8subj (pamap2). 15 of 38 groups hit the 20x cap; the fewest-subject capped groups are exactly these tiny single-dataset pools. So the core claim — 20x oversampling collides with tiny subject pools, enabling subject memorization — is confirmed, as is the fix rationale (cap by n_subjects). Caveats that temper it: (1) the finding's kuhar examples are weaker than stated — push_up/sit_up/talking draw from 36/41/71-73 subjects (kuhar has 89), not a handful; the concern is really carried by dsads/mhealth/pamap2. (2) Total-subject figure differs (I get 397 recoverable vs the finding's 367 capped-corpus) — minor. (3) The asserted zero-shot/cross-subject HARM is a plausible mechanism but not demonstrated; several oversampled tiny-pool groups (elevator, ironing, mhealth exercises) are absent from the held-out eval taxonomy, so direct eval impact is unquantified. Net: mechanism and headline evidence confirmed; MEDIUM is honest given the harm is real-but-unmeasured and the fix is cheap. || UNCERTAIN(repro): The structural mechanism is real: compute_group_weights (multi_dataset_loader.py:775-847) does not use subject counts, and MAX_OVERSAMPLE_RATIO=20.0 is passed at semantic_alignment_train.py:2253. Subject-count compensation is genuinely absent. However, four specific refutation points significantly narrow the scope and severity of the finding:

1. The "20x oversampling" figure is wrong in practice. The 20x parameter is the maximum allowed weight ratio between the rarest and most-common per-sample weight, not the actual oversampling multiple over naive sampling. Because the largest group (sitting/walking) has 61,500 windows, the cap threshold is groups with fewer than 3,075 windows — and every group below that threshold gets exactly the same capped weight. The result is that ALL named rare groups are boosted to exactly 5.2x over naive (uniform) sampling, not 20x. They are still well below the balanced-per-group ideal of 2.38% each (elevator: 0.62%, arm_exercise: 0.22%). The finding conflates the weight-ratio cap parameter with the actual observed oversampling multiple.

2. The subject counts are materially wrong for four of the seven named kuhar groups. The finding implies these are "handful of people" pools: measured counts are push_up=36 subjects, sit_up=41 subjects, talking_sitting=71 subjects, talking_standing=73 subjects — not small pools. Only mhealth (arm/leg/waist exercise, 10 subjects each) and dsads elevator (8 subjects) are genuinely tiny. The finding's kuhar examples do not support the memorization risk it claims for them.

3. Zero of the named rare groups appear in any of the six held-out eval label sets (confirmed by grepping all benchmark_data/eval_v2/labels/*.json). The claim that oversampling "undermines cross-subject and zero-shot performance for them" is entirely untestable under the current protocol: the classes at issue are train-only. No eval metric deterioration attributable to this mechanism can be observed.

4. The combined effective training mass of the genuinely tiny-pool groups (mhealth arm/leg/waist exercise + dsads elevator) is 1.3% of the effective training distribution (each at 0.22–0.62%). Per-patch z-score normalization strips per-subject DC offset and amplitude, which is the primary substrate for subject-level memorization of IMU signals. The proposed subject-count cap would further reduce exposure to groups that already sit well below the balanced-per-group target.

The residual concern is limited to two source datasets (mhealth, dsads) whose rare groups are backed by 8–10 subjects and are drawn at 5.2x the naive rate, contributing 1.3% of effective training mass. The mechanism is real and the proposed cap is defensible as a design improvement, but the finding's evidence overstates both the oversampling multiple (5.2x not 20x effective) and the subject-pool sizes for the kuhar groups, and the claimed generalization harm cannot be measured under the current evaluation protocol since none of these groups appear in any test set.
